1

Please identify the members of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel.
2 
A.
KEDNY and KEDLI’s (collectively, the “Companies”) Gas Infrastructure and Operations
3 
Panels (collectively, “Panel”) consist of Ross W. Turrini, Aaron J. Choo, Patty McVeigh,
4 
Pradheep Kileti, and Srividya Madhusudhan.
5

Is this the same Panel that testified previously in these proceedings? 
7 
A.
Yes.  Capitalized terms defined in the Panel’s direct and corrections and updates testimony 
8 
have the same meanings here.

10 
Q.
What is the purpose of the Panel’s rebuttal testimony? 
11 
A.
The purpose of the Panel’s rebuttal testimony is to respond to certain recommendations set 
12 
forth in the prepared testimony of the Department of Public Service Staff (“Staff”) Gas 
13 
Infrastructure and Operations Panel (“SGIOP”), the Staff Pipeline Safety Panel (“SPSP”), 
14 
the Staff Gas Reliability and Supply Panel (“SGRSP”), and the Staff Policy Panel (“SPP”), 
15 
the City of New York’s witness John Sano (“NYC Witness Sano”) and New York City 
16 
Policy Panel (“NYCPP”) (collectively, “NYC”), Alliance For a Green Economy 
17 
(“AGREE”), and Sane Energy Project (“SANE”). 
18 
19 
The Panel’s testimony will address recommendations and proposed adjustments to the 
20 
Companies’ forecast incremental gas capital and operations and maintenance (“O&M”) 
21 
expenditures, including:

3 
address the recommendations of NYC and SANE to disallow investments for 
4 
growth-related system expansion and reinforcements;

II.
Staff’s General Approach to Capital Programs Adjustments
14 
Q.
Please summarize the SGIOP’s general approach and basis for proposed adjustments
15 
to the Companies’ capital forecasts.
16 
A.
The SGIOP generally supports the Companies’ capital plans (at 20) but proposes
17 
adjustments for several programs in the Customer Connections, Mandated, Reliability, and
18 
Non-Infrastructure categories.  The SGIOP offers the following general justifications for
19 
its proposed adjustments:
20 
•
Where the Companies’ forecasts were derived using historic averages of spending,
21 
the SGIOP (at 29, 44, 45, and 60) believes the forecasts should exclude FY 2021
22 


--- Page 6 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 4 of 54 
from the three-year average because of the impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic on 
1 
contractor and construction costs.   
2 
•
The SGIOP (at 31-32) recommends adjustments to the Companies’ Gas System
3 
Reinforcement Program in certain categories because specific projects have not
4 
been identified in in the Data Years.
5 
•
The SGIOP (at 54) proposes adjustments to the Companies’ Service Replacements
6 
– Non-Leaks, Reactive Mains, and Inactive Service forecasts because there have
7 
been historic variances between the Companies’ budgets and actual spending in 
8 
these categories.   
9 
10 
Q.
Have the Companies provided adequate information to the SGIOP and other parties 
11 
to enable a thorough review of the proposed capital investment plan?   
12 
A.
Yes.  The Companies’ direct testimony and exhibits provided extensive information for 
13 
each program and project, including data sheets (Exhibit __ (GIOP-5)) that contain project 
14 
descriptions, projects needs and justification, forecasting methodologies, cost drivers, 
15 
applicable regulatory requirements, and alternatives analyses.  The Companies also 
16 
responded to nearly 400 IRs in these proceedings regarding the details of the capital plan.  
17 
18 
Q.
Does the Panel agree with the SGIOP’s methods and justifications for its proposed 
19 
adjustments? 
20 
A.
No, the SGIOP’s proposed adjustments are not supported by sufficient data and analysis in 
21 
several cases.  For example, the SGIOP’s high-level adjustment to remove FY 2021 from 
22 
the average historic cost calculation (because it appears relatively higher) does not consider 
23 


--- Page 7 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 5 of 54 
 
the specific factors impacting the Companies’ spending in that year.  To reduce the 
1 
Companies’ rate allowance in certain areas, the SGIOP selectively chooses the historic 
2 
years used to calculate its unit cost forecast for different cost components – even within 
3 
the same category (e.g., Mandated).  In some cases, the SGIOP argues to include more 
4 
recent data because it was a better indicator of the Companies’ Rate Year costs, while in 
5 
other cases the SGIOP argues that more recent data – when showing increases – should be 
6 
normalized out.  This approach reduces the Companies’ rate allowance by inconsistently 
7 
including or excluding historic data, which the Panel believes is unreasonable. 
8 
 
9 
Next, the SGIOP removes funding in categories where specific projects have not yet been 
10 
identified in the Data Years without considering the Companies’ planning process for these 
11 
categories and the need to address emerging system conditions in the outer years to 
12 
maintain safety and reliability.  The Panel describes why certain programs are needed to 
13 
maintain flexibility to identify specific projects in the future.   
14 
 
15 
Finally, the SGIOP’s focus on historic actual-to-budget variances in individual capital 
16 
categories fails to recognize that the Companies manage their capital spend across the 
17 
entire portfolio, not just individual categories or projects, which can create variances in 
18 
given years based on operational priorities and emerging system conditions.   
19 
  
20 
Q. 
Please describe how the Companies determined their proposed capital forecasts. 
21 
A. 
The Companies determined the appropriate basis and methodology for forecasting Rate 
22 
Year and Data Year investments on a program-by-program basis.  Following a detailed 
23 


--- Page 8 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 6 of 54 
 
analysis of each program, including examination of historic spending, the Companies 
1 
determined whether an average of historic project or unit costs represented the best 
2 
predictor of Rate Year costs for each program.  The Companies also identified projects for 
3 
which a project estimate, rather than a forecast based solely on historic costs, was more 
4 
appropriate.  In this way, the Companies carefully considered any anomalies prior to setting 
5 
its forecasts for each program.  The Companies’ methodologies for deriving program 
6 
forecasts are provided in the Companies’ testimony and exhibits and in response to 
7 
numerous information requests. 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
How will the SGIOP’s proposals impact the Companies’ ability to provide safe and 
10 
reliable service? 
11 
A. 
Adopting the SGIOP’s recommendations would hinder the Companies’ ability to deliver 
12 
on many of the Companies’ and the Commission’s important policy, environmental, 
13 
reliability and safety objectives.  Specifically, proposed downward adjustments to unit 
14 
costs in the Mandated category will challenge the Companies’ ability to deliver on 
15 
aggressive goals for LPP replacement and methane reductions.  Proposed adjustments in 
16 
the Reliability category will compromise the Companies’ ability to replace aging facilities 
17 
and equipment that are critical to system operations.  The Companies appreciate the 
18 
SGIOP’s concerns for managing increasing costs; however, the reality is that the SGIOP’s 
19 
proposed adjustments will result in the reprioritization of capital investments in a manner 
20 
that fails to achieve the Companies’ and the Commission’s goals.  This is not the right 
21 
result from a public policy, reliability, or safety perspective.  The Panel discusses in more 
22 


--- Page 9 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 7 of 54 
 
detail the impacts of Staff’s and intervenors’ recommendations and proposed adjustments 
1 
to individual capital programs in the sections that follow.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please discuss the SGIOP’s recommendation to remove FY 2021 from the historic 
4 
three-year average cost calculation is several program categories.   
5 
A. 
In several instances where the Companies’ forecasts were derived using historic averages 
6 
of spending, notably the Gas System Reinforcements and Proactive Main Replacement 
7 
(LPP) programs, the SGIOP proposed to exclude FY 2021 from the three-year average as 
8 
an “outlier” because it believes this year was artificially high due to the impacts of COVID-
9 
19.  However, the proposed adjustments are based on a misconception of the factors driving 
10 
costs in FY 2021, and simply removing that year from the historic costs results in an 
11 
artificially low unit cost that would prevent the Companies from delivering these important 
12 
programs (especially when coupled with Staff’s proposal to significantly increase LPP 
13 
retirement mileage over the next several years).   
14 
 
15 
While a project may begin in one fiscal year, it may be completed in a different fiscal year, 
16 
depending on project complexity and the construction schedule.  Also, invoices are paid 
17 
throughout the project lifecycle as work components are completed in the field (initially 
18 
main installation invoices, then services invoices, later paving invoices), but a project’s 
19 
retirement units are only recorded when the job is fully complete and in-service, which can 
20 
occur in a different fiscal year than paid invoices. For these reasons, looking at a single 
21 
year is typically unhelpful when developing unit-cost forecasts, whereas using a three-year 
22 
average smooths the impacts of these timing issues.   
23 


--- Page 10 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 8 of 54 
 
In this case, contrary to Staff’s suggestion, the Companies did not experience higher unit 
1 
costs in FY 2021 because of the COVID-19 pandemic increasing contractor and 
2 
construction costs.  However, the pandemic did extend the time between project unit 
3 
completion and invoice payment due to construction and invoicing delays.  Because of 
4 
safety considerations, the Companies focused on work that involved limited customer 
5 
interactions, such as LPP, in FY 2021.  Costs were incurred for LPP projects and partial 
6 
invoices were paid in FY 2021 (main installation and services work) but scheduling delays 
7 
resulted in fewer units being completed in FY 2021 and more units being completed in FY 
8 
2022.  This has the compounding effect of making FY 2021 unit costs appear relatively 
9 
higher and FY 2022 unit costs appear relatively lower.  Therefore, removing FY 2021 from 
10 
the three-year average artificially lowers the Companies’ average unit costs.  Indeed, the 
11 
current trending average unit costs align with the unit costs presented in the Companies’ 
12 
forecast.  
13 
 
14 
Q. 
Please address SGIOP’s proposed adjustment to the FY 2024 capital budgets. 
15 
A. 
The SGIOP (at 10) proposes approximately $23.8 million of adjustments to the Companies’ 
16 
capital forecasts for FY 2024 (the Companies’ current fiscal year).  The FY 2024 capital 
17 
budgets were approved by the Commission as part of the 2019 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate 
18 
Cases.  The Companies are currently executing their capital workplans based on these 
19 
approved budgets and, once the assets are placed into service, the associated costs are being 
20 
collected through the Net Utility Plant surcharge.  Therefore, the Companies do not believe 
21 
it is appropriate to retroactively adjust FY 2024 and the Commission should reject any 
22 
attempt to re-open previously approved capital budgets.  
23 


--- Page 11 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 9 of 54 
 
III. 
Proposed Adjustments to Customer Connections Programs 
1 
A. Customer Connections  
2 
Q. 
Please summarize the SGIOP’s recommended adjustments to the Companies’ forecast 
3 
of new units/connections in the Customer Connections Programs.  
4 
A. 
The SGIOP (at 27) generally agrees with the Companies’ methodology for deriving the unit 
5 
costs in the Customer Connections area but recommends adjustments to the forecast for new 
6 
units/connections and makes corresponding adjustments to the Install Main, Install 
7 
Services, and Install Meter/Regulator programs.  The Companies forecast the number of 
8 
units based on the recent experience with new customer connections in FY 2022 (discussed 
9 
more below), then made adjustments based on factors impacting future growth and gas 
10 
saturation in the service territories.  The Companies also made downward adjustments to 
11 
the number of new connections based on the anticipated impacts of known regulatory 
12 
changes affecting new gas connections the Rate Year and Data Years, specifically 
13 
restrictions and prohibitions on certain gas connections resulting from New York City’s 
14 
Local Law 154 (prohibiting on-site combustion of fossil fuels in new buildings (with 
15 
exceptions) beginning in 2024).  Instead of using the most recent customer connections 
16 
data, the SGIOP (at 27) recommends using a three-year average of the number of services 
17 
installed from FY 2020 to FY 2022, which the SGIOP suggests “captures more of a trend 
18 
the Companies have experienced historically for customer connections.”  The SGIOP 
19 
argues that the proposed adjustments for the pending legislative/regulatory changes is not 
20 
appropriate because there is no experience operating under these conditions and, therefore, 
21 
it is difficult to predict the potential impacts on new connections.    
22 
 
23 


--- Page 12 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 10 of 54 
 
Q. 
What is the Companies’ response to this recommendation? 
1 
A. 
The Companies maintain that their approach to forecasting the number of new connections 
2 
based on recent data (FY 2022) is a more nuanced and informed view, as compared to using 
3 
a three-year average, considering the recent trends in new connections and anticipated 
4 
impacts from the regulatory/legislative changes.  First, given various economic and market 
5 
factors that can influence the number of customer connections, the Companies believe 
6 
current customer and market data is the best indicator of near-term demand for new 
7 
connections.  Next, given New York City’s restrictions on certain new gas connections are 
8 
expected to take place during the Rate Year (i.e., are known), and the Companies can make 
9 
reasonable assumptions on the likely impacts given the types of service connections that 
10 
will be impacted (i.e., are measurable), the Companies believe their approach produces a 
11 
more informed forecast for new gas connections in the future.   Indeed, there are too many 
12 
dynamic factors influencing future growth on the system to rely on a simple, three-year 
13 
average.  
14 
 
15 
Additionally, the Companies’ recent experience with new applications supports the view 
16 
that Staff’s forecast of new units/connections is understated.   For example, KEDNY 
17 
received 2,622 and 2,506 new applications in FY 2022 and FY 2023, respectively.  This 
18 
exceeds the SGIOP’s adjusted forecast based on a historic three-year average (2,336 
19 
connections/year).  Several market factors are likely contributing to this trend, including the 
20 
cost of alternative heating options/fuels and increased customers interest in submitting 
21 
applications for new gas service prior to the effective date of the new restrictions.  This 
22 


--- Page 13 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 11 of 54 
 
recent data suggests the Companies’ approach is a better indicator of demand for future 
1 
connections.    
2 
 
  
3 
Q. 
How are the Companies’ addressing the lack of operating experience with the 
4 
upcoming restrictions on new gas connections (Local Law 154)?   
5 
A. 
As the Panel explained in its direct testimony, to address the uncertainty created by the 
6 
phased implementation of restrictions on the number of new gas connections, the 
7 
Companies propose to continue the existing two-way tracker for their respective Customer 
8 
Connections programs.  While the SGIOP did not address this proposal in its testimony, a 
9 
two-way tracker will ensure that the level of funding matches the number of new 
10 
connections in the Rate Year and Data Years, protecting both the Companies and customers 
11 
from any material deviations between the forecasts and actual results.      
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Please comment on NYC’s proposal to eliminate all funding for Customer Connections 
14 
from the capital budget. 
 
15 
A. 
NYC Witness Sano (at 3) proposes eliminating “all proposed capital spending in the 
16 
Customer Connections category in furtherance of the requirements under New York’s 
17 
Climate Leadership & Community Protection Act (‘CLCPA’) and related City laws.”  This 
18 
is an unreasonable proposal for several reasons.  As we explained in our direct testimony, 
19 
the Companies are required by the Public Service Law, the Commission’s regulations, and 
20 
the Company’s tariff to extend service to new customers in its service territory.  While the 
21 
phased implementation of restrictions on gas connections in New York City will have an 
22 
impact on the number of gas connections in the future, the NYC acknowledges the 
23 


--- Page 14 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 12 of 54 
 
restrictions will occur over time, and there are numerous exceptions that will still require 
1 
the Companies to connect new customers (e.g., oil-to-gas conversions).   The Companies 
2 
have attempted to address the impact of the pending restrictions through downward 
3 
adjustments to the growth forecast (KEDNY 39.4 percent and KEDLI 20.6 percent) but 
4 
eliminating all program costs for customer connections unfairly requires the Companies to 
5 
fund new connection costs and establish regulatory deferrals that would need to be collected 
6 
in the future.  The Companies’ approach to have the capital budgets reflect a reasonable 
7 
amount of funding for customer connections based on an informed forecast, and a true-up 
8 
mechanism for costs based on actual experience, is better from both a ratemaking and 
9 
affordability standpoint.  Given that the Companies will have a continuing obligation to 
10 
connect new customers during the period covered by these rate cases, it would be 
11 
unreasonable to preclude recovery of the costs incurred in fulfilling that obligation.   
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Please comment on the potential impacts of the All Electric Building Act?  
14 
A. 
As noted by NYC Witness Sano, the State of New York’s “All Electric Building Act” 
15 
(included in Part RR of Chapter 56 of the Laws of 2023) restricts gas connections statewide 
16 
in new construction for buildings with fewer than seven stories beginning in 2026 and taller 
17 
buildings beginning in 2029.  Like Local Law 154, the law does not impact connections to 
18 
existing buildings (e.g., oil-to-gas conversions) and there are numerous exceptions for 
19 
connections to new construction (e.g., hospitals, critical infrastructure, and commercial food 
20 
establishments).   Because the All Electric Building Act (i) will become effective after Local 
21 
Law 154 (which will impact most of the service territory (by customer count) and is already 
22 
reflected in the unit forecast); (ii) does not impact customer connections in the Rate Year; 
23 


--- Page 15 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 13 of 54 
 
(iii) will not impact potential applicants in larger buildings until after the term of the 
1 
proposed rate plans; and (iv) includes exceptions that will continue to require the 
2 
Companies to connect new customers, the Companies do not believe further adjustment to 
3 
the Customer Connections forecast is warranted at this time.  Additionally, to the extent the 
4 
All Electric Buildings Act reduces customer connections beyond the Companies’ adjusted 
5 
forecast, the associated cost reductions would be reflected in the Companies’ proposed 
6 
tracker for new customer connections.    
7 
 
8 
B. Gas System Reinforcements 
9 
Q. 
Please address the SGIOP’s proposed adjustments (at 29-31) to the Companies’ Gas 
10 
System Reinforcement Programs in the Rate Year. 
11 
A. 
For the Gas System Reinforcement Program, the Companies used a three-year average unit 
12 
cost from FY 2020 to FY 2022 to develop the Rate Year forecast.  Again, the SGIOP (at 
13 
29-30) proposes to adjust the unit cost to remove FY 2021 because of the perceived impact 
14 
of the pandemic, proposing to instead utilize a three-year average comprised of FY 2019, 
15 
FY 2020, and FY 2022.   As the Panel discusses above, removing FY 2021 from a three-
16 
year average understates the unit cost and compromises the Companies’ ability to deliver 
17 
this critical program.   
18 
 
19 
Q. 
Please address the SGIOP’s proposed adjustments (at 30-32) to the Gas System 
20 
Reinforcement Program in the Data Years. 
21 
A. 
The SGIOP recommends significant reductions to the Companies’ forecast Gas System 
22 
Reinforcement budgets in FY 2026 to FY 2028 because the specific projects in these years 
23 


--- Page 16 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 14 of 54 
 
have not yet been identified.  Instead, the SGIOP proposes budgets for FY 2026 to FY 2028 
1 
using the forecast for FY 2025 adjusted for inflation.  The SGIOP’s recommendation is not 
2 
informed by any specific analysis.   
3 
 
4 
The Gas System Reinforcement Program projects are required to maintain pressure above 
5 
system minimums on the gas network during periods of peak demand, thereby maintaining 
6 
continuous service to all gas customers.  The low-pressure network is very sensitive to 
7 
changes in demand and, as such, reinforcement projects on the low-pressure network are 
8 
only identified two years out.  As the demand forecast and gas system hydraulic models are 
9 
updated each year, reinforcement projects are developed to address emerging needs on the 
10 
system. Thus, the low-pressure project budget placeholders are necessary to account for 
11 
projects that will be identified in future years.   While high-pressure systems are generally 
12 
more resilient to forecast changes, placeholders for high-pressure reinforcement projects 
13 
are also necessary to support the Companies’ LPP replacement programs.   
14 
 
15 
In summary, while these reinforcement projects have yet to be identified in the outer years, 
16 
the Companies’ operating experience indicates the need for reinforcements going forward 
17 
– especially as there is continued growth on the system.   The SGIOP’s recommendation 
18 
may jeopardize the Companies’ ability to ensure reliable service and meet its obligations to 
19 
serve increasing demand from existing customers.   
20 
 
21 


--- Page 17 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 15 of 54 
 
IV. 
Proposed Adjustments to Mandated Programs 
1 
A. City State Construction (“CSC”) Program 
2 
Q. 
Please explain the SGIOP’s adjustments to KEDNY and KEDLI’s CSC/Public Works 
3 
Reimbursable forecasts. 
4 
A. 
The SGIOP (at 40) agrees with the Companies’ general methodology for forecasting the 
5 
CSC budgets, but Staff recommends imputing approximately $18.3 million and $1.3 million 
6 
to KEDNY and KEDLI’s CSC Reimbursable forecasts, respectively, based on an 
7 
expectation that the Companies will collect some amount of reimbursements from New 
8 
York City under the Cost Sharing Agreement (“CSA”) (until its expiration on June 30, 
9 
2025). The SGIOP’s proposed adjustments are based on a calculated historical 
10 
reimbursement percentage of 5.68 percent for KEDNY and 10.81 percent for KEDLI.   The 
11 
SGIOP (at 41) acknowledges that the Companies have not received reimbursements from 
12 
New York City since FY 2020; nonetheless, Staff believes it is appropriate to impute some 
13 
level of reimbursements because “New York City is required to reimburse the [Companies] 
14 
for work related to the CSC projects.”     
15 
 
16 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with Staff’s adjustments to its CSC Program? 
17 
A. 
No.  Given the Companies’ significant challenges securing reimbursements from New York 
18 
City, which Staff concedes have resulted in zero reimbursements over the last three years, 
19 
the Companies believe the CSC program forecast appropriately excluded any level of 
20 
assumed reimbursement under the CSA.   
21 
 
22 


--- Page 18 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 16 of 54 
 
In response to Staff’s IR No. DPS-NYC-1 (Exhibit __ (GIOP-1R)), New York City 
1 
indicated that outstanding reimbursements under the CSA, which amount to more than $175 
2 
million, are currently the subject of “settlement discussions between the City of New York 
3 
and National Grid.”   
4 
 
5 
Given the Companies are already at risk of under recovering millions in reimbursements for 
6 
past work under the CSA, the fact that no reimbursement payments have been made in 
7 
years, and the fact that these unremunerated reimbursements have been built into rates, it is 
8 
simply unreasonable to assume the Companies will be reimbursed for an amount of CSC 
9 
expenditures going forward.  To the extent the Companies are successful in securing 
10 
reimbursements in the future, the funds will be credited back to customers through the CSC 
11 
capital tracker (discussed below).  Therefore, the Companies proposal to exclude 
12 
reimbursements from the CSC forecast fairly balances the need to fund this mandated 
13 
program while ensuring customers will receive the full benefit of any reimbursements 
14 
through the tracker mechanism.   
15 
 
16 
Q. 
Does the current true-up mechanism for CSC expenditures address KEDNY’s 
17 
concern that the program will be under funded if Staff’s recommendation to impute 
18 
CSA reimbursements is accepted? 
19 
A. 
No.  Under the Joint Proposal adopted in the 2019 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases, 
20 
KEDNY and KEDLI have a reconciliation mechanism for CSC capital expenditures.  To 
21 
the extent that Companies’ actual capital spending for CSC, net of reimbursements, differs 
22 
from the forecast amount in a Rate Year, the Companies can defer the revenue requirement 
23 


--- Page 19 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 17 of 54 
 
effect (excluding O&M expenses) associated with 90 percent of the difference for future 
1 
recovery from or return to customers.  This mechanism provides some protection against 
2 
the volatility in the CSC process due to changing New York City plans and the timing lag 
3 
between rate setting and New York City’s year-to-year project construction determinations.  
4 
As stated in the Panel’s direct testimony, however, a partial deferral mechanism is not a 
5 
substitute for appropriate rate recovery.  The Companies must be permitted to recover 
6 
enough in rates to fully fund the necessary work of this mandated program.  The Companies’ 
7 
forecasts are based on known factors regarding the state of CSA reimbursements, whereas 
8 
the reconciliation is intended to cover for unknown cost increases.  Therefore, having a 
9 
reconciliation in place does not blunt the detrimental effects of removing nearly $20 million 
10 
from the Companies’ CSC program forecasts.   
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Does the SGIOP testimony address the Companies’ proposal to modify their current 
13 
reconciliation mechanism to allow deferral of 100 percent of the difference between 
14 
forecast and actual CSC capital expenditures and to add a reconciliation mechanism 
15 
for CSC-related O&M?   
16 
A. 
The SGIOP (at 108-109) does not support the Companies’ proposal to create a fully 
17 
reconciling capital deferral mechanism for CSC costs.  While acknowledging that CSC 
18 
work “is reactive to the municipal projects and certain aspects of this program are beyond 
19 
the Companies’ control,” Staff argues that the Companies are still responsible for managing 
20 
the projects and contractors who ultimately complete the work and, therefore, a fully 
21 
reconciling mechanism is not appropriate.  For the same reasons, Staff opposes a 
22 
reconciliation for CSC-related O&M costs.  
23 


--- Page 20 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 18 of 54 
 
As stated above, the capital reconciliation mechanism is intended to manage the aspects of 
1 
CSC that are not within the Companies’ control, including the unpredictability of the work 
2 
that will ultimately be required each year.  Because this work is mandated and beyond the 
3 
Companies’ control, as acknowledged by Staff, there is simply no basis for the mechanism 
4 
to provide less than a full reconciliation for this mandated capital work. 
5 
 
6 
Moreover, the SGIOP states no justification for disallowing a CSC-related O&M 
7 
reconciliation mechanism considering that the Companies have demonstrated significant 
8 
volatility in O&M spending year-on-year.  Even if the Companies manage this program to 
9 
maximize efficiencies and controllable costs, the inability to predict future program needs 
10 
would still justify a reconciliation mechanism.   
11 
 
12 
In summary, the Companies’ CSC programs present unique challenges in the ability to 
13 
accurately forecast, and, for that reason, capital and O&M reconciliation mechanisms are 
14 
appropriate to protect both customers and the Companies.    
15 
 
16 
B. Proactive Main Replacement Program (Leak Prone Pipe)  
17 
Q.  
Please describe the SGIOP’s adjustments to the Companies’ forecasts for their 
18 
Proactive Main Replacement Programs. 
19 
A. 
The Companies used a three-year cost history (FY 2020 to FY 2022) and applied an 
20 
inflation factor to develop the unit cost for the planned proactive main replacements (LPP) 
21 
in the Rate Year.  The SGIOP (at 44-45) generally agrees with the Companies’ forecasting 
22 
methodology for LPP replacement unit costs but recommends several adjustments.  First, 
23 


--- Page 21 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 19 of 54 
 
the SGIOP proposes to remove costs incurred in FY 2021 from the unit cost calculation 
1 
under a theory this year was an “outlier” due to allegedly inflated costs resulting from the 
2 
pandemic.  Next, the SGIOP adjusted the mileage targets for this program based on the 
3 
recommendation from the SPSP to increase both Companies’ annual LPP retirements. 
4 
(SGIOP at 45).  Finally, the SGIOP increased the number of LPP replacement miles 
5 
assumed to be achieved through the CSC Program and other capital programs.  (SGIOP at 
6 
46). 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Do the Companies agree with Staff proposed unit cost adjustments? 
9 
A. 
No.  For the reasons discussed above, removing FY 2021 from the three-year average is not 
10 
a reasonable approach to calculating historic unit costs.   In addition, the Panel observes the 
11 
following issues with Staff’s unit cost calculation: 
12 
• 
Staff’s calculation does not provide funding for the additional miles Staff 
13 
assumes will be achieved through the CSC program; 
14 
• 
Staff’s calculation did not provide funding for five additional miles included in 
15 
KEDLI’s CY 2027 mileage targets; and  
16 
• 
Staff’s funded targets and minimum replace targets are not aligned, as was 
17 
Staff’s intention.  As discussed by the Companies’ Gas Safety Panel, the 
18 
Companies believe that the annual minimum replacement targets should be five 
19 
miles less than the funded targets each calendar year (with a cumulative target 
20 
at the end of the rate plan), consistent with the current metric structure.   
21 


--- Page 22 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 20 of 54 
 
Therefore, the SGIOP’s adjustment will underfund this program and jeopardize the 
1 
Companies’ ability to keep pace with targets and goals for LPP removal, and the associated 
2 
methane emission reductions.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Please address the recommendations regarding the annual mileage targets in the 
5 
Proactive Main Replacement (LPP) Program?   
6 
A. 
Staff’s recommendations regarding the LPP replacement targets, metrics, incentives, and 
7 
reporting are addressed by the Companies’ Gas Safety Panel’s Rebuttal Testimony.  As 
8 
discussed in that testimony, Staff’s proposed LPP mileage targets for KEDNY and KEDLI 
9 
are approximately 40 percent and 20 percent higher, respectively, than the already 
10 
aggressive targets proposed by the Companies.   
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Does the SGIOP’s adjustments include the incremental O&M costs from the increased 
13 
mileage? 
14 
A. 
No.  In addition to the capital component of main replacement, there is an O&M component 
15 
for the cost to disconnect customers from the retired main and reconnect customers to the 
16 
new main (referred to as disconnects and reconnects, or “D&Rs”).  The SGIOP’s 
17 
adjustments did not include the cost for the additional D&Rs that would be required given 
18 
the significant increase in proposed LPP mileage.  Additionally, the SGIOP does not include 
19 
the costs associated with the O&M component of the incremental resource coordinators, 
20 
project managers, engineers, and other personnel that would be required to deliver a 
21 
significantly expanded main replacement program.   
22 
 
23 


--- Page 23 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 21 of 54 
 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with the SGIOP’s adjustments regarding the number of miles 
1 
forecast to be retired from the CSC Program and other capital programs? 
2 
A. 
No.  The SGIOP (at 36) developed a ratio of total CSC expenditures to LPP miles replaced 
3 
based on actual costs in FY 2021 to FY 2023, and then applied this ratio to its forecast CSC 
4 
budgets to project the mileage of LPP expected to be replaced through the CSC program.  
5 
This back-of-the-envelope approach is flawed for several reasons.  The ability to retire LPP 
6 
through the CSC program depends on the size, type, and location of the municipal 
7 
construction projects in the service territory.  Because of the inherent difficulties predicting 
8 
the scope and scale of CSC work, it is not reasonable to assume that a simple ratio of historic 
9 
spend to LPP miles will predict future opportunities to retire additional LPP from the CSC 
10 
or other capital program.  For example, large CSC projects may not produce any LPP 
11 
replacements depending on the impacted area of the distribution system.  Therefore, the 
12 
SGIOP’s proposal is this area should be rejected.       
13

C. Pipeline Integrity Program 
15 
Q. 
Please describe the SGIOP’s proposed adjustments to the Companies’ Pipeline 
16 
Integrity Programs. 
17 
A. 
The SGIOP (at 48) proposes to adjust the forecast for KEDLI’s Pipeline Integrity – 
18 
Northwest Nassau Transmission Main & Control Valve – Phase 3 Project.  While 
19 
acknowledging KEDLI provided a “detailed” estimate that allocated costs by fiscal year 
20 
based on activities such as project development and design, permitting, resource 
21 
availability, long-lead material purchases, gas-outage windows, construction schedules, and 
22 
project need dates, the SGIOP nonetheless proposes to adjust the forecast based on a 
23 


--- Page 24 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 22 of 54 
 
comparable project, Northwest Nassau Phase 2.  Using the Phase 2 costs, adjusted for 
1 
inflation, and increased by five percent to account for the increased mileage of Phase 3, the 
2 
SGIOP proposes adjustments of $7.7 million in the Rate Year, $8.25 million in Data Year 
3 
1, and $8.622 million in Data Year 2. 
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with the proposed adjustment to the Northwest Nassau 
6 
Transmission Main & Control Valve – Phase 3 Project? 
7 
A. 
No.  The SGIOP fails to explain why using costs for a comparable, but entirely separate and 
8 
distinct, project are a superior approach to using a detailed estimate for the actual project.  
9 
Costs can vary between these types of projects for any number of reasons, including the 
10 
location of facilities, work schedules and restrictions, soil type and drilling requirements, 
11 
permitting requirements, etc.   
12 
 
13 
Indeed, Northwest Nassau Phase 3 differs from Phase 2 in several respects.  First, Phase 3 
14 
will be performed largely at night along the north service road of the Long Island 
15 
Expressway, while Phase 2 was completed during the day and on roads with less traffic 
16 
congestion.  The Phase 3 location will require KEDLI to utilize additional traffic control 
17 
and protection.  Night work will increase costs due to collective bargaining rules dictating 
18 
higher rates for night work by employees, as well as increase the costs to open asphalt plants 
19 
and other critical places after hours.  Also, excavation depths and sheeting requirements 
20 
will differ for the two phases of the project, as each installation crosses different utilities 
21 
and obstructions.  Finally, since the time Phase 2 was sent out for bid, contractor costs 
22 
overall have increased significantly, which will drive higher costs for Phase 3.   
23 


--- Page 25 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 23 of 54 
 
In summary, KEDLI’s detailed assessment of the actual costs to complete this project is a 
1 
far more accurate approach than an approach using a comparable project as a proxy, which 
2 
fails to consider any job-specific factors impacting costs.  For this reason, the SGIOP 
3 
adjustments should be rejected.    
4

VII. 
Advanced Communications Infrastructure (“ACI”) Category  
14 
Q. 
Please described Staff’s recommendation for the ACI Program? 
15 
A. 
The ACI Program will fund development of a detailed scope of work and business plan to 
16 
deploy an interconnected network to facilitate the installation of smart meters and methane 
17 
detection devices that will provide real-time safety benefits and enable additional metering 
18 
functionality.  ACI consists of three components: (i) an ACI Network Program, (ii) an AMI 
19 
Residential Methane Detector (“RMD”) Program, and (iii) AMI Smart Meter Replacement 
20 
Program proposals.   
21 
 
22 


--- Page 31 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 29 of 54 
 
The SPP (at 71) opposes the Companies’ proposal to deploy an ACI Program on the 
1 
grounds the “Companies are unable to demonstrate that the benefits of their AMI program 
2 
proposals justify the costs.”  The SPP’s position is largely based on fact that the Companies 
3 
have not yet developed detailed Benefit Cost Analyses (“BCA”) for the project.   The SPSP 
4 
proposes an alternative to the RMD component of the ACI Program that would use 
5 
connected RMDs and make use of a Long-Range Wide Area Network, similar to the RMDs 
6 
used by Con Edison in its service territory.   
7 
 
8 
Q. 
What is the Panel’s response to the SPP’s recommendation to reject the ACI Network 
9 
and Smart Meter proposal at this time? 
10 
A. 
The SPP, while citing the testimony that explains the approach to develop a detailed 
11 
deployment plan and cost analysis in the first two years of the program, still appears to 
12 
misconstrue the Companies’ approach to this project.   
13 
 
14 
 
The Companies’ Gas Safety Panel explained that the AMI Network Program includes two 
15 
phases: Phase One involves a detailed analysis of AMI network options and development 
16 
of a detailed business case and information technology (“IT”) plan.  The Companies 
17 
requested incremental O&M costs of $1 million annually in the Rate Year and Data Year 
18 
1 to complete this work.  If Phase One is approved, Phase Two will involve upgrading IT 
19 
back-office system integrations and deployment of a field area network to support the 
20 
program.  
21 
 
22 


--- Page 32 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 30 of 54 
 
Therefore, while it is true the Companies’ do not yet have detailed BCAs for the project, 
1 
the Companies described that the initial funding for the project would be used to develop 
2 
this type of analysis as part of a longer-term deployment plan for the ACI Network and 
3 
AMI Smart Meter Replacement Program.  Given the SPSP’s interest in deploying 
4 
connected RMDs in the Companies’ service territories, the Commission should support 
5 
funding for Phase I and II of this project.   
6 
 
7 
Q. 
What is the Panel’s response to the SPP and SPSP’s alternate proposal to the 
8 
Companies’ RMD proposal? 
9 
A. 
As explained in the Gas Safety Panel’s rebuttal testimony, Staff’s proposal is not workable 
10 
because, unlike Con Edison who has installed an AMI network in its service territory, the 
11 
Companies do not have access to a network that would allow for the deployment of 
12 
connected RMDs.

19

20 
• New RNG sources can help offset the need for additional upstream pipeline 
21

22 
constrains; and 
23 


--- Page 33 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 31 of 54

1

2 
goals of a 40 percent reduction in greenhouse gas emissions from 1990 by 2030 
3 
and 85 percent by 2040. 
4 
Q. 
What are the SGRSP’s recommendations regarding the Companies’ RNG 
5 
Interconnection Projects? 
6 
A. 
The SGRSP (at 14-19), Staff makes the following recommendations regarding the 
7 
Companies’ RNG Interconnection Projects: 
8 
• Due to the uncertainty of the Companies’ RNG Interconnection Projects’ in-service 
9 
date, the Commission should remove these projects from the capital budget and 
10 
projected plant in-service in this rate proceeding and, instead, allow the Companies 
11 
to recover the revenue requirement for these projects through a surcharge 
12 
mechanism if they are built and placed into service during the Rate Year; 
13 
• The surcharge mechanism should be limited to the revenue requirement impacts of 
14 
the current estimated costs of the Companies’ RNG Interconnection projects; 
15 
• Similar to the requirements instituted in Con Edison’s gas rate proceeding (Case 
16 
22-G-0065), the Commission should require the Companies to file a report 90 days 
17 
prior to construction of any RNG interconnection project containing information 
18 
on: the project cost estimate, reliability benefits to the system in the vicinity of the 
19 
project, the RNG source materials, and a detailed accounting of the upstream GHG 
20 
emissions avoided by the RNG; 
21 
• Costs associated with the purchase of RNG should be addressed through the Gas 
22 
Adjustment Statement; 
23 


--- Page 34 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 32 of 54 
 
• The Companies should not purchase the environmental benefits associated with 
1 
RNG; and 
2 
• Staff does not support the three incremental FTEs proposed by the Companies to 
3 
support the Companies’ RNG Interconnection Projects. 
4 
Q. 
Do the Companies agree with any of the SGRSP’s recommendations regarding the 
5

In part.  The Companies agree with the SGRSP’s recommendations to file a report 90 days 
7 
prior to construction of any RNG Interconnection Project and that costs associated with 
8 
purchase of RNG be accomplished through the Gas Adjustment Statement.  
9 
 
10 
Q. 
What is the Companies’ position on Staff’s other recommendations regarding the 
11 
RNG Interconnection Projects? 
12 
A. 
The Companies do not agree that recovery of the revenue requirement for the Companies’ 
13 
RNG Interconnection Projects should be accomplished through a surcharge mechanism if 
14 
the projects are built and placed into service during the Rate Year. The Companies have 
15 
been working with the developers and, due to the Inflation Reduction Act (“IRA”) that 
16 
requires these projects commence construction prior to December 31, 2024, are optimistic 
17 
that the developers can deliver an RNG interconnection project as scheduled to meet the 
18 
IRA timeline. 
19 
 
20 
The Companies also disagrees that recovery of the revenue requirement for the RNG 
21 
Interconnection Projects be limited to the current estimated costs without accounting for 
22 


--- Page 35 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 33 of 54 
 
unanticipated cost increases for labor and materials.  Recent years have seen unprecedented 
1 
issues with supply chain and increases in costs of both construction labor and materials.  
2 
 
3 
Next, the Companies believe that they should be permitted to purchase environmental 
4 
attributes of RNG.  Although the Companies are not proposing to procure the 
5 
environmental attributes from the RNG Interconnection Projects, the adoption of a clean 
6 
fuel standard, or other change in market conditions, may necessitate the Companies to 
7 
procure environmental attributes of alternative fuels during the term of the rate plan.  
8 
Therefore, the Companies need to maintain the flexibility to purchase environmental 
9 
attributes of RNG or other alternative fuels and the Commission should not tie the 
10 
Companies’ hands from being able to make these decisions when beneficial to customers.  
11 
 
12 
Finally, the Companies require additional staffing in this area: to perform engineering 
13 
analysis; support application of standards, procedures and state/federal requirements in 
14 
system designs; collaborate with internal and external stakeholders to enable safe and 
15 
reliable interconnections of renewables into gas system. The SGRSP also supports further 
16 
exploration of RNG interconnections as NPAs for capacity constrained areas that will be 
17 
supported by these incremental FTEs.

B. 
Hydrogen Pilot (HyGrid) 
20 
Q. 
Please provide an overview of the SGRSP testimony as it relates to KEDLI’s hydrogen 
21 
blending pilot known as the HyGrid project. 
22 


--- Page 36 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 34 of 54 
 
A. 
The SGRSP initially notes the CAC Scoping Plan’s acknowledged the need to further study 
1 
the use of hydrogen as an alternative fuel to help decarbonize difficult sectors. Staff offers 
2 
the following additional comments regarding the HyGrid Project (at 26-31): 
3 
• Due to the nascent nature of hydrogen blending programs in New York State, the 
4 
SGRSP is not familiar with the permeability of existing in-service pipeline 
5 
materials by hydrogen and associated safety considerations;  
6 
• Odorant used should not interact with the hydrogen blended gas so as to render the 
7 
odorant ineffective, become toxic, or cause harm to the integrity of the system;  
8 
• Leak detection devices might not appropriately detect or alarm for a blend; 
9 
especially if there’s variations in the concentrations of the blend; and  
10 
• The HyGrid project is not far enough in design to meet the September 2024 in-
11 
service date. 
12 
Q. 
What are the SGRSP’s recommendations regarding the HyGrid Project? 
13 
A. 
The SGRSP makes the following recommendations regarding the HyGrid Project: 
14 
• The HyGrid Project should be delayed for one year and the requested FTEs for the 
15 
project should be removed from the revenue requirement for the Rate Year; 
16 
• The Commission should set a cap on the capital costs of the HyGrid Project and 
17 
any additional pilot costs above the cap should be funded by shareholders; 
18 
• Further detailed study is required to establish permeability rates; 
19 
• Any pilot program will need to adequately address these safety concerns and any 
20 
other operational considerations caused by the introduction of hydrogen into the 
21 
gas system; and 
22 


--- Page 37 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 35 of 54 
 
• Blending should not occur on segments of the system with known leaks or on 
1 
pipeline materials known to be leak prone. Specifically, the SGRSP recommends 
2 
that that any hydrogen blending only occur in plastic or protected steel segments 
3 
and that the Companies take affirmative steps to inspect and repair any and all 
4 
known leaks prior to any blending on such segments. 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
Do the Companies agree with the SGRSP’s recommendations summarized above 
7 
regarding the HyGrid Project? 
8 
A. 
The Companies agree with the SGRSP’s recommendations with two exceptions. First, even 
9 
though the SGRSP recommends delaying the in-service date of the HyGrid Project by one 
10 
year, the Companies will need to hire the proposed FTEs, one in FY 2024 and the second 
11 
in the Rate Year (FY 2025), to pursue engineering and design; support application of 
12 
standards, procedures and state or federal requirements in system designs; permitting; 
13 
conduct community and customer outreach; coordinate with the Town of Hempstead; and 
14 
conduct the procurement process for equipment and construction of the HyGrid Project. 
15 
Second, the Companies disagree with capping the capital costs of the HyGrid Project.  As 
16 
noted above, in recent years the world has experienced unprecedented supply chain 
17 
problems and increased costs for construction labor and materials. It is appropriate to allow 
18 
recovery for cost increases attributable to inflation, supply chain issues, and similar 
19 
unanticipated items.  
20 
 
21 
 
22 
 
23 


--- Page 38 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 36 of 54 
 
Q. 
Please address the SGRSP’s recommendations regarding the safety of hydrogen 
1 
blending. 
2 
A. 
National Grid agrees that safety is paramount, and it is KEDLI’s intention that any efforts 
3 
to introduce hydrogen into the Company’s infrastructure or its customers’ equipment will 
4 
be performed with safety as the number one consideration.  The Company has examined 
5 
the issues Staff identified prior to proposing the HyGrid project.  National Grid’s frequently 
6 
asked questions (“FAQ”) document, “The Critical Role of Hydrogen in Our Clean Energy 
7 
Future” (December 2022) (“Hydrogen FAQ”), included in IR No. NRDC-27, Attachment 
8 
1, a copy of which is provided in Exhibit ___ (GIOP- 1R), addresses the safety 
9 
considerations associated with hydrogen blending. 
10 
 
11 
  
As summarized in the Hydrogen FAQ, gas systems in the U.S., including some portions of 
12 
the Companies’ networks, have safely distributed hydrogen before, primarily in the form 
13 
of manufactured gas, which had as much as 50 percent hydrogen blend by volume flowing 
14 
through the system.  Hawaii Gas Company currently delivers residential customers with 
15 
gas containing up to 15 percent by volume hydrogen.  In addition, there are over 1,600 
16 
miles of dedicated hydrogen pipeline currently operating in the U.S., including three miles 
17 
in New York State.  As the Hydrogen FAQ notes, a recent study also found that gas 
18 
infrastructure that does not leak when carrying natural gas likewise will not leak when 
19 
carrying hydrogen. The piping in the Point Lookout neighborhood into which hydrogen 
20 
will be blended is non-LPP.  That was one of the primary reasons for selecting this location 
21 
for the HyGrid pilot.  
22 
 
23 


--- Page 39 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 37 of 54 
 
National Grid is working with technical and industry partners on research projects covering 
1 
personnel and customer safety, pipeline integrity, material capabilities, gas blending, 
2 
metering and measurement technologies, leak detection, and appliance performance.  
3 
These efforts include work with the U.S. Department of Energy and four of their national 
4 
labs, as well as with NYSERDA and Stony Brook University. 
5 
In summary, hydrogen is a safe fuel when correctly implemented, and National Grid will 
6 
take all appropriate measures for the safety of our communities, customers, and employees.     
7 
 
8

10

11

12 
December 15, 2023 before any project can advance to Stage 2.  These FTEs will be 
13

14

15 
will not address cost recovery in Case 22-M-0429 until much later in calendar year 2024. 
16 
Further, approval of FTEs in this proceeding will not impact the cost recovery mechanisms 
17 
for the UTEN projects the Commission ultimately adopts in Case 22-M-0429. This 
18 
approach is similar to incremental FTEs for energy efficiency programs, where the 
19 
incremental FTEs are approved in the Companies’ base rate cases but the energy efficiency 
20 
budgets are reviewed and approved in the New Efficiency New York (“NE:NY”) 
21 
proceeding (Case 18-M-0084).

IX. 
Operator Qualifications (“OQ”) Program Category 
1 
Q. 
Please discuss Staff’s proposed adjustments to the OQ program.   
2 
A. 
As explained by the SGIOP, the Companies’ forecast OQ Program budgets are based on 
3 
historic spending with specific adjustments to incorporate the additional training and 
4 
performance evaluations requirements based on the Commission’s “Memorandum and 
5 
Resolution Adopting Amendments to 16 NYCRR 1 Part 255,” issued on March 18, 2022, 
6 
in Case 19-G-0736 (the “OQ Order”).  The Companies forecast includes “Contract 
7 
Increases,” which captures the increased OQ requirements for contractors, based on annual 
8 
contract costs multiplied by a percentage increase determined by the total number of work 
9 
types impacted by the increased OQ requirements.   
10 
 
11 
For the Mains and Services and Complex Projects category, the Companies assumed an 
12 
increase of five percent to capture the additional OQ costs associated with this work.  The 
13 
SGIOP (at 77) takes the position that the Companies “failed to fully explain or justify the 
14 
assumption of the five percent minimum increase in costs for these types of projects.”   
15 
Instead, the SGIOP proposes to use the two percent increase for expanded OQ requirements 
16 
the Companies used for other project categories, such as Service Line, Leak Survey, and 
17 
Damage Prevention.    
18 
 
19 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with the SGIOP’s proposed adjustments? 
20 
A. 
No.  As acknowledged by the SGIOP, the Companies developed a forecast of increased 
21 
OQ costs based on an assessment of the OQ requirements for each work category.  The 
22 
five percent increase for Main and Services and Complex Construction is justified because 
23 


--- Page 42 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 40 of 54 
 
this work involves more OQ requirements than other work (e.g., Service Line).  For 
1 
example, Mains and Service contractors require 20 to 30 qualifications to perform 
2 
functions such as installing main, replacing services, and performing tie-ins.  The more 
3 
complicated the construction task, the more OQs the individual worker is required to hold.  
4 
A foreman conducting a tie-in (connecting live gas mains), one of the most complex 
5 
construction functions, is required to hold 40 or more OQs, with each OQ requiring 
6 
separate training and testing.  By comparison, functions that are less complex, such as 
7 
locating and inspecting facilities for Service Line Inspections or Damage Prevention, 
8 
require workers to carry only two OQs.   
9 
 
10 
Rather than apply a single percentage increase to all work categories, the Companies 
11 
undertook to identify the anticipated impacts of the Commission’s expanded OQ 
12 
requirements on specific projects.  This approach produces forecast that better reflect the 
13 
anticipated cost increase for OQ compliance and, therefore, should be used to develop 
14 
budgets in this area. 
15 
 
16

Indirect Category

 The SGIOP recommends removal of the following Indirect projects for KEDNY: (1)

removal of the following Indirect projects for KEDLI: (1) Melville Hub Asset Renewal –

3 
 
4 
Q. 
What does the SGIOP explain as the basis for the removal of these projects? 
5 
A. 
The SGIOP (at 84) expresses concern with the Companies’ use of a fit-out guide issued 
6 
by its consultant, Jones Lang LaSalle (“JLL”), as a means of developing project costs.  
7 
The SGIOP (at 89) also question the Companies’ ability to complete projects included in 
8 
its budget based on an average historic underspend over a three-year period from FY 2021 
9 
to FY 2023 relative to budget in that same period.   
10 
Q. 
Do the Companies agree with the SGIOP assertion (at 85) that utilizing the JLL fit-
11 
out guide is an unreliable way to budget projects? 
12 
A. 
No.  Benchmark costs in the JLL fit-out guide are developed with input from thousands 
13 
of projects managed by JLL across North America.  The guide relies on actual projects to 
14 
develop average costs by region and provides them specifically for pre-project planning 
15 
purposes.  Despite the variability of materials, existing space conditions, and final layout 
16 
all have on a final project budget, the fit-out guide is intended to provide a mid-point for 
17 
scoping a project’s potential costs that takes hard costs, soft costs (design and fees for 
18 
architecture, engineering, project management, consulting, etc.), furniture, fixtures, and 
19 
equipment, and potential tenant requirements (information technology, audio/visual, 
20 
security, etc.) into account.  The Staten Island 200 and Melville Hub projects are the type 
21 
of projects that the JLL fit-out guide is intended to inform, i.e., projects in the pre-planning 
22 
phase with general office refurbishment requirements captured in the fit-out guide’s cost 
23 


--- Page 44 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 42 of 54 
 
calculations.  Similarly, budgets for the L&D Pipeline OQ Training Center projects for 
1 
both KEDNY and KEDLI, and the Instrumentation and Regulation (“I&R”) Training 
2 
facility for KEDLI, were informed by the JLL fit-out guide for budgeting purposes 
3 
because of their similarity to the types of projects JLL uses to develop its fit-out guide.   
4 
 
5 
Q.  
The SGIOP states (at 89) that historic underspend demonstrates the Companies’ 
6 
inability to deliver the Greenpoint Infrastructure project.  Does the Panel have 
7 
additional information to refute that? 
8 
A. 
Yes.  In the winter of 2022, as project complexity and needs increased, the Companies 
9 
began utilizing the services of JLL to support its facilities function.  JLL support includes 
10 
developing project budgets and scopes, overseeing projects underway, and providing 
11 
valuable insight into facilities management best-practices.  As a result, the Companies’ 
12 
facilities team has been able to meet the needs of the various business functions it supports 
13 
and is better equipped to meet its budget, a trend anticipated to continue.  In FY 2023, the 
14 
first full fiscal year after JLL began supporting the Company’s facilities team, facilities 
15 
spend exceeded the budget by nearly 400 percent for KEDLI and was at 89 percent of 
16 
budget for KEDNY.  As shown on Exhibit ___ (GIOP-2R), facilities spend for KEDLI in 
17 
FY 2023 was $3.784 million against a budget of $0.950 million, and in the same period 
18 
facilities spend for KEDNY was $21.221 million against a budget of $23.855 million.  
19 
Taking JLL’s support into account, and based on FY 2023 spend to budget, the 
20 
Companies do not see underspending its facilities budgets as a basis for reducing its 
21 
capital forecast.  
22

Q.

A.

Q.

1 
A.

3

4

5

6

E. Resource Coordinators 
1

2

and scheduling.  Without these incremental resources, the Companies will not be able to

16 
A. Capitalization of Cast Iron (“CI”) Joint Repairs (NYC) 
17 
Q. 
What is NYC’s position on the Companies’ proposal to capitalize all joint repairs on 
18 
large diameter CI pipes that are sixteen inches and larger? 
19 
A. 
NYC Witness Sano (at 28) proposes that the Commission should review the proposed 
20 
changes to the capitalization policy for joint repairs on large diameter CI to decide if this 
21 
work is appropriately included in rate base, while also noting the work would otherwise be 
22 
recovered as O&M expense. 
23 


--- Page 51 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 49 of 54 
 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with this recommendation? 
1 
A. 
No, this work is appropriately capitalized because it extends the useful life and integrity of 
2 
the asset.  The Panel’s direct testimony also explained that repairing joint seals is the most 
3 
cost-effective way to address leaks on large diameter CI mains, while also avoiding the 
4 
public disruptions from full main replacements.  (Exhibit___ (GIOP-5, pages 23-26)).  
5 
NYC witness Sano does not suggest the proposed accounting change is inconsistent with 
6 
accounting rules or the Commission’s regulations.  The SGIOP notes that the change would 
7 
align the capitalization policies with the previously approved treatment for CISBOT and 
8 
pipe lining, and also align with the capitalization policies for similar work in other 
9 
jurisdictions.  For these reasons, the City’s recommendation should be rejected.     
10

B. LPP Unit Cost Incentive 
12 
Q. 
What is the Companies’ position on the SGIOP’s opposition to the LPP unit cost 
13

14 
A. 
The SGIOP (at 111) opposes the Companies’ proposed LPP unit cost incentive because it 
15

16 
achieving meaningful efficiencies and Staff believes there are already appropriate 
17 
incentives to manage costs.  The Panel does not agree.  The LPP unit cost productivity 
18 
incentive is an appropriate mechanism to encourage better cost management for the 
19 
proactive LPP retirement program, assuming the targets are set appropriately based on 
20 
current unit costs.   
21

D. Greenpoint LNG Investments 
6 
Q. 
Please address SANE (at 12-17) and AGREE’s (at 17-24) recommendations that the 
7 
Commission should consider more flexible phasing-in of the investment in the 
8 
Greenpoint LNG Plant, if it is not completely disallowing these projects. 
 
9 
A. 
As the Panel discusses in detail in its direct testimony, KEDNY maintains on-system 
10 
supply through its LNG facility at the Greenpoint LNG Plant. The plant is a critical 
11 
component of KEDNY’s gas supply portfolio and gas operating network and plays a 
12 
critical role in meeting peak demand.  Winter Storm Elliot (December 23) highlighted the 
13 
reliability concerns and the risks of relying on increasingly constrained/unreliable upstream 
14 
assets.  LNG assets provided needed on-system reliability benefits during these 
15 
increasingly frequent events. 
16 
 
17 
 
The Companies have answered numerous information requests explaining the need for 
18 
upgrades to the LNG plant.  These investments are supported by the SGIOP and NYC 
19 
Witness Sano.  SANE and AGREE’s recommendation that the projects can be deferred or 
20 
avoided is unsupported and, therefore, should be rejected.

E.  Non-Pipes Alternatives (“NPAs”) 
1 
Q. 
Please address NYC’s contention that the Companies are not doing enough to 
2 
implement NPAs. 
3 
A. 
The NYCPP (at 8) contends that National Grid has not done enough to advance NPAs in 
4 
its service areas, and that the Companies must be more proactive in promoting and 
5 
implementing NPAs.  The Companies acknowledge that they have not yet identified a 
6 
suitable NPA; however, they reject the suggestion that they are not actively seeking to find 
7 
and implement NPAs.   
8 
 
9 
In August 2022, the Companies submitted NPA screening and suitability criteria in the Gas 
10 
Planning Proceeding (Case 20-G-0131).  The Companies have incorporated the NPA 
11 
screening process into their capital planning processes to identify potential alternatives to 
12 
traditional solutions, where such alternatives promote safe and reliable service, satisfy 
13 
benefit-cost considerations, and can mitigate GHG emissions.  This NPA screening process 
14 
is documented in the project papers the Companies filed in these proceedings (KEDNY 
15 
Exhibit __ (GIOP-5) and KEDLI Exhibit __ (GIOP-5)).   
16 
 
17 
The Companies also have committed to continue looking for opportunities to advance 
18 
NPAs in the Rate Year and Data Years and propose to continue to annually submit a request 
19 
for proposal for NPA solutions and to identify five LPP projects that could be replaced 
20 
with NPAs.  This will include looking for NPAs in Disadvantaged Communities; however, 
21 
the Companies do not agree that prioritizing Disadvantaged Communities when searching 
22 
for NPA opportunities will necessarily lead to identifying more NPAs or more beneficial 
23 


--- Page 54 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 52 of 54 
 
NPAs.  As NYC acknowledges, NPAs generally require the agreement of all customers 
1 
affected by the proposed alternative, and the Companies cannot unilaterally impose an 
2 
NPA solution on customers without their cooperation.  
3 
 
4 
Finally, while the Companies contend their approach to NPAs is reasonable, we 
5 
nevertheless will continue to consider ways to improve the process to identify and 
6 
implement appropriate NPAs and will incorporate any requirements or guidance on NPAs 
7 
that come from the Gas Planning Proceeding.

to fail in the field and must make the necessary investments to the replace these units for 
1 
the foreseeable future.   
2 
   
3 
G.  Roadway Excavation Quality Assurance Act 
4 
Q. 
What is the Roadway Excavation Quality Assurance Act? 
5 
A. 
The Roadway Excavation Quality Assurance Act (S.4887/A.5608) (the “Act”), which was 
6 
signed into law by the Governor on August 16, 2023, mandates prevailing wages for 
7 
construction workers on roadway excavation projects. Specifically, the Act requires utility 
8 
company contractors and subcontractors on a “covered excavation project” to pay 
9 
prevailing wages to laborers, workers, and mechanics. A “covered excavation project” 
10 
means a construction project where a permit is issued by the state, county, or municipality 
11 
to use, excavate, or open a street.  A prevailing wage is either: (i) the rate set for the trade 
12 
or occupation in the locality where the covered excavation occurs, or (ii) the rate set by the 
13 
prevailing scale of union wages on excavation projects where local law or ordinance 
14 
requires only competent workers be employed on the projects.  The Act applies to 
15 
contracts/permits issued on or after September 15, 2023.  The Act will increase the costs 
16 
of the Companies’ street construction work performed by contractors and subcontractors.  
17 
Given the volume of street construction work necessary to operate and maintain the 
18 
Companies’ underground networks, these incremental costs will be material. 
19 
 
20 
 
21 
 
22 
 
23 


--- Page 56 ---
Rebuttal Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
Page 54 of 54 
 
Q. 
What is the Companies’ proposal for the incremental costs to comply with the Act? 
1 
A. 
Given these costs are material and will impact the Companies in the Rate Year and Data 
2 
Years, the Companies propose that they be allowed to defer the full costs associated with 
3 
implementation of the Act during the term of the rate plan for future recovery.   
4 
 
5

7

8

KEDLI’s facilities on eastern Long Island are critical to ensuring the availability of life-
9

10 
does not provide any analysis that would support the position that KEDLI can retire gas 
11 
facilities in this area.

funding to complete reactive non-leak work activities outside the normal scope of the
Company’s integrity, reliability, public works, and growth programs.  Examples of work

service inactivity or customer demolition requests, customer-driven relocations of
existing services, and substandard conditions discovered during Company field work and
inspections.  This program is reactive and the annual workplan can vary based on
customer requests received and the number of substandard conditions identified in the
field.

Clean hydrogen will play an important role in the decarbonization of the Northeast. It has the potential to decarbonize multiple 
sectors of the economy by providing a cleaner residential heating fuel, an energy resource for industries that are hard to  
decarbonize, a zero-carbon transportation fuel for larger vehicles and an on-demand, carbon-free fuel for power generation. 
•
A low-cost solution to a clean energy future: Hydrogen will allow for a balanced and resilient pathway to
decarbonization, requiring less buildout of new assets and ultimately minimizing the impact on customer energy bills,
all while achieving regional net zero goals. By delivering a blend of hydrogen and renewable natural gas (RNG) in
existing networks, customers can access clean heat without making costly investments in new electric appliances or
upgrading their electrical systems. This is especially important for renters who don’t have the option to upgrade.
•
A safe alternative. Incorporating hydrogen into the energy mix is part of our plan to eliminate fossil fuels from
our systems by 2050 or sooner. It is a clean and safe fuel that will begin to ramp up throughout the decade and
eventually scale to meet the needs of the communities we serve. The U.S. gas network has seen hydrogen before.
Prior to the mid-1950s before the advent of natural gas, some gas systems including our own networks had as
much as 50 percent hydrogen blend flowing in the system.
•
Improved resiliency and reliability of the entire energy system. Because hydrogen can be stored for long
periods of time (analogous to battery storage of electricity), it can be used to generate electricity and heat, and it
will help to decarbonize the integrated gas and electric system.
We understand our customers and stakeholders have questions about the use of hydrogen and the role it 
will play going forward, and we have compiled the following Q&A to address many of these topics.
National Grid’s Hydrogen Vision
1. What is clean hydrogen and why is it important to a clean energy future?
Clean hydrogen is a renewable fuel that can be produced emissions-free from water and renewable electricity.
This process is part of a nearly-perfect renewable cycle; electricity splits water into hydrogen and oxygen, and when
hydrogen is used as a fuel, it combines with oxygen to form water, emitting no carbon dioxide. Clean hydrogen is
valuable because it can store energy from renewable sources like solar and wind, either for using directly as a fuel
or for generating clean power at another time.
We believe that clean hydrogen is key to a clean energy future and to ensuring we fully eliminate fossil fuels from our 
electric and gas system by 2050. It plays two major roles: 
•
Clean hydrogen will help remove emissions from sectors that are hard to decarbonize like heat, certain industries,
and heavy transport. In many cases, fully electrifying these sectors would be prohibitively expensive or technically
not feasible. Hydrogen provides a clean, carbon-free fuel for these sectors that will replace the carbon-intensive
fuels they use today.
•
By using clean hydrogen to fuel power generation, we will make a fully-renewable power grid more reliable with less
infrastructure build-out (transmission lines, substations, etc.). During times when renewables like solar and wind
The critical role of

are producing less power than is needed (for example, when winds are low or the sky is cloudy), on-demand 
power generation using clean hydrogen will fill the gaps. And when renewables are making more power than 
can be used, clean hydrogen can be produced from excess renewable energy and stored for later use in 
power generation.  
2. How and when does National Grid expect to integrate hydrogen into our gas networks?
National Grid is a leader when it comes to delivering clean energy solutions to our customers. We are proactively
finding ways to help society transition to a low-carbon and ultimately carbon-free future through research and
development, and planning for the evolution of our networks to deliver carbon-free fuels.
Our vision for hydrogen is to create an ecosystem that includes blending hydrogen with renewable natural gas 
into the existing gas networks, developing 100% hydrogen-fueled use cases for campuses and specific buildings, 
creating hydrogen clusters anchored by large commercial and industrial customers in a distinct network of hydrogen 
users, and fueling our power generators.
We are accelerating the energy transition through pilot demonstrations in the next five years to position our assets 
and operational processes to safely deliver low-carbon energy, like hydrogen. By 2030, we intend to scale up 
low-carbon energy significantly in conjunction with our existing capital investment plans. With increasing availability 
of clean hydrogen over time, we will increase the volume of hydrogen blends to displace fossil gas delivered to our 
customers, with plans to have 20% hydrogen on our gas networks by 2040.1  In certain sections of our networks, 
we will deploy 100% hydrogen clusters for customers and communities that will cater to diverse needs. This 
includes energy usage delivered through fuel cells, district heating, and other uses.
Displacing fossil fuel with hydrogen will be implemented concurrently with our existing programs of energy 
efficiency, demand response, renewable natural gas, and electrification of heat. Integrating hydrogen with other 
programs will safeguard the reliable energy that our customers depend on.
In parallel, we are replacing existing gas distribution networks across our service territory as part of long-term  
programs to modernize its infrastructure, ensure public safety and reduce methane emissions. These programs plan 
to replace all leak-prone mains by the time high-hydrogen blends are expected to be available and economical.   
3. What has National Grid done to ensure that hydrogen is safe?
Working with partners such as Northeast Gas Association’s NYSEARCH, GTI Energy, Operations Technology
Development (OTD) and Utilization Technology Development (UTD), and other joint industry collaboratives, National
Grid has supported nearly 40 research projects covering topics such as personnel and customer safety, pipeline
integrity, material compatibility, gas blending, metering and measurement technologies, leak detection and
quantification, customer appliance performance, and other end use applications. These studies, along with
numerous other scientific studies conducted and published by other entities around the world, have given us
insight into how hydrogen will behave in our networks and how we can implement hydrogen in a way that is
safe for our customers, employees, and equipment. Specific hydrogen safety topics are explored in the next
section of this document.
•
We are working with the U.S. Department of Energy and four of their national laboratories on the Pipeline
Blending CRADA – a HyBlend Project, which seeks to address technical barriers to blending hydrogen in
natural gas pipelines.
•
We are working with the New York State Energy and Research Development Authority (NYSERDA) and Stony
Brook University to understand how hydrogen will perform in our existing gas infrastructure.
•
We are introducing one of the first and largest clean hydrogen blending projects in the country in the town
of Hempstead, Long Island, which will heat 800 homes and fuel a fleet of city vehicles.
•
We are partnering with the Standard Hydrogen Corporation (SHC) to build and operate SHC’s Energy
Transfer System, the nation’s first multi-use, renewable hydrogen-based energy storage and delivery system,
in New York. This project has been approved by New York’s Public Service Commission and will have the

ability to provide clean energy services to National Grid and its customers, including renewably charging 
electric vehicles, refueling long-range fuel cell electric vehicles, providing long-duration backup power, and
